Class field theory for curves over local fields
Amalendu Krishna, Subhadip Majumder

Abstract




2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 14C35; Secondary , 19F05

Abstract.

We establish a ramified class field theory for smooth projective curves over local
fields. As key steps in the proof, we obtain new results in the class field theory
for 2-dimensional local fields of positive characteristic, and
prove a duality theorem for the logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology on
affine curves over local fields.

1.
Introduction

The class field theory for smooth projective curves over local fields
is now well understood following the works of Bloch
[
3
]
,
Saito
[
40
]
, Kato-Saito
[
25
]
and Yoshida

[
45
]
. The prime-to-characteristic case of the class field theory for
open smooth curves was studied by Hiranouchi
(cf.
[
12
]
,
[
13
]
). The objective of this paper is
to extend the class field theory results for smooth projective curves over
local fields to open curves in full generality. We achieve this by proving
new results in the class field theory of 2-local fields of positive charateristics,
and extending the duality theorem of Kato-Saito
[
25
]

for the p-adic étale cohomology of smooth projective curves over local fields
to open curves over such fields. We describe the main results below.

1.1.
Main results

We fix a local field
k
of characteristic
p>0
and let
X
be a connected,
smooth and projective curve over
k
. Let
D\subset X
be an effective divisor on

X
with complement
X^{o}
. The class group with modulus
C(X,D)
and
the class group
C(X^{o})
were introduced by Hiranouchi

[
13
]
1
1

1

We use a slightly different definition of
C(X^{o})

without affecting the reciprocity homomorphism.

both of which coincide with the class group
SK_{1}(X)
, used in
[
40
]

when
D
is empty. Hiranouchi also defined the abelian étale fundamental group
with modulus
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X,D)
and constructed the reciprocity homomorphisms

\rho_{X^{o}}\colon C(X^{o})\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{o})
and
\rho_{X|D}\colon C(X,D)\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X,D)
. All of these are recalled in §
5
.
One can endow
C(X^{o})
and
C(X,D)
with the structure of
topological abelian groups such that
\rho_{X^{o}}
and
\rho_{X|D}
are continuous.
We let
C(X,D)_{0}
denote the kernel of the norm map

C(X,D)\to k^{\times}
(cf. §
5
) and define
C(X^{o})_{0}
similarly.
We prove the following.

Theorem 1.1
.

For the reciprocity map
\rho_{X|D}
, we have the following.

(1)

\rho_{X|D}\colon{C(X,D)}/n\to{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X,D)}/n
is a monomorphism
for every integer
n\geq 1
.

(2)

{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X|D})
is the maximal divisible subgroup of
C(X,D)
.

(3)

The image of the map
\rho_{X|D}\colon C(X,D)_{0}\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X,D)
is not
necessarily finite.

(4)

There exists an integer
r\geq 0
depending only on
X
and
an exact sequence

0\to({{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})^{r}\to{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X,D)}^{\star}\to C(X,D)^{\star}_{\rm tor}\to 0.

Theorem 1.2
.

For the reciprocity map
\rho_{X^{o}}
, we have the following.

(1)

\rho_{X^{o}}\colon{C(X^{o})}/n\to{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{o})}/n
is a monomorphism
for every integer
n\geq 1
.

(2)

{\rm Ker}(\rho_{X^{o}})
is the maximal divisible subgroup of
C(X^{o})
.

(3)

The image of the map
\rho_{X^{o}}\colon C(X^{o})_{0}\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{o})
is not
necessarily finite.

(4)

There is an exact sequence (with
r\geq 0
as in Theorem
1.1
)

0\to({{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})^{r}\to{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{o})}^{\star}\to C(X^{o})^{\star}_{\rm tor}\to 0.

As a consequence of item (4) of the above results, one obtains that there is a
one-to-one correspondence between the following two sets.

(1)

Finite abelian covers of
X^{o}
(resp. with ramification
bounded by
D
away from
X^{o}
) in which not all closed points of
X^{o}

are completely split.

(2)

Open subgroups of finite index in
C(X^{o})
(resp.
C(X,D)
).

The items (1), (2) and (4) of the above theorems
are extensions of what one knows in the class field
theory for
X
.
On the other hand, the items (3) of the above two results are in contrast
with the case of
X
. The prime-to-
p
parts of (2)
in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
as well as
the
r=0
case of item (4) of Theorem
1.2

were shown by Hiranouchi
[
13
]
.

We let
\widehat{C}(X^{o})={\varprojlim}_{D}C(X,D)
, where the limit is taken over
all effective divisors on
X
supported on the complement of
X^{o}
.
One knows that
\rho_{X^{o}}
actually factors through

C(X^{o})\to\widehat{C}(X^{o})\xrightarrow{\widehat{\rho}_{X^{o}}}\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{o})
.
Some authors use
\widehat{C}(X^{o})
as the definition of the class group of
X^{o}
.
The next result proves the analogue of Theorem
1.2
for
\widehat{\rho}_{X^{o}}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

For the reciprocity map
\widehat{\rho}_{X^{o}}\colon\widehat{C}(X^{o})\to\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{o})
,
we have the following.

(1)

\widehat{\rho}_{X^{o}}\colon{\widehat{C}(X^{o})}/n\to{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{o})}/n
is a monomorphism
for every integer
n\geq 1
.

(2)

{\rm Ker}(\widehat{\rho}_{X^{o}})
is the maximal divisible subgroup of
C(X^{o})
.

(3)

There is an exact sequence (with
r\geq 0
as in Theorem
1.1
)

0\to({{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})^{r}\to{\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X^{o})}^{\star}\to\widehat{C}(X^{o})^{\star}_{\rm tor}\to 0.

In addition to the class field theory for
X
, the key ingredients in the proofs of
Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
are the next two results.
The first is concerning the class field theory of
2-local fields. To state it, we fix some notations. Let
K
be a Henselian discrete
valuation field of characteristic
p>0
whose residue field is a local field.
Let
G^{\rm ab}_{K}
denote the abelianized absolute Galois group of
K
and let

K_{*}(K)
denote the Milnor
K
-theory of
K
.
Kato
[
19
]
defined a reciprocity homomorphism

\rho_{K}\colon K_{2}(K)\to G^{\rm ab}_{K}
. Unlike the case of local fields,
this map has a non-trivial kernel. However, Kato showed that
K_{2}(K)

is endowed with a topology with respect to which
\rho_{K}
is continuous
and the dual map
\rho^{\vee}_{K}\colon(G^{\rm ab}_{K})^{\star}\to K_{2}(K)^{\star}
is bijective.
We prove the following result about
\rho_{K}
.

Theorem 1.4
.

For every integer
n\geq 1
, we have the following.

(1)

\rho_{K}\colon{K_{2}(K)}/n\to{G^{\rm ab}_{K}}/n

is a monomorphism.

(2)

\rho_{K}\colon~_{n}K_{2}(K)\to~_{n}G^{\rm ab}_{K}
is an epimorphism.

An immediate consequence of Theorem
1.4
is that
{\rm Coker}(\rho_{K})
is
torsion-free. It also implies that
{\rm Ker}(\rho_{K})
is
divisible, a difficult result of Fesenko
[
4
]
.
On the other hand,
{\rm Coker}(\rho_{K})
is not divisible (cf. Proposition
4.10
),
a phenomenon which is in contrast with the class field theory of local fields.

We now describe the second ingredient, a duality theorem.
We let
m\geq 1
be an integer and let
W_{m}\Omega^{0}_{X|D,{\operatorname{log}}}
denote the complex

[Z_{1}W_{m}{\rm fil}_{D}W_{m}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\xrightarrow{1-C}{\rm fil}_{D}W_{m}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]
on
X_{\text{\'{e}t}}
,
where the sheaf
W_{m}{\rm fil}_{D}W_{m}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
was introduced in

[
26
, § 3]
and
C
is the Cartier operator (cf. §
6.1
).
For
i\geq 1
, we let
W_{m}\Omega^{i}_{X|D,{\operatorname{log}}}
be the logarithmic Hodge-Witt sheaf on

X_{\text{\'{e}t}}
introduced in
[
17
, § 1]
. We endow the (hyper)cohomologies of

W_{m}\Omega^{0}_{X|D,{\operatorname{log}}}
and
W_{m}\Omega^{i}_{X|D,{\operatorname{log}}}
with the structure of
topological abelian groups.
For a topological abelian group
G
, let
G^{\rm pf}
denote the profinite
completion of
G
. We prove the following.

Theorem 1.5
.

For every
i\geq 0
, there is a perfect pairing of topological abelian groups

H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,W_{m}\Omega^{2}_{X|D,{\operatorname{log}}})^{\rm pf}\times H^{2-i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,W_{m}\Omega^{0}_{X|D,{\operatorname{log}}})\to{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}}.

This theorem was proven by Kato-Saito
[
25
]
when

D=\emptyset
in which case the profinite completion is redundant.
We show however that taking the profinite completion is essential in
Theorem
1.5
if
D\neq\emptyset
.
The above duality is compatible with the inclusion of divisors
D\subseteq D^{\prime}
so
that taking the limit and colimit, we get a duality theorem for
the analogous cohomology groups on
X^{o}
(cf. Theorem
7.12
).
In a forthcoming paper
[
30
]
, we shall extend the duality
theorem to the cohomology groups of
W_{m}\Omega^{1}_{X|D,{\operatorname{log}}}
.

1.2.
Overview of proofs

The main technical results which allow us to prove Theorems
1.1

and
1.2
are Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
.
To prove Theorem
1.4
, we first describe Kato’s reciprocity
homomorphism in terms of a duality map between Hodge-Witt forms.
We then use the classical local
Grothendieck duality and a topological duality result for vector spaces over a
local field equipped with the adic topology of the field to show that this
duality is perfect. This is a key step which makes things work and
allows us to conclude the injectivity of the
local reciprocity map with finite coefficients.
A technical result in ramification theory that we also need in the process is
Proposition
2.8
. The next three sections of this paper are devoted to the
proof of Theorem
1.4
.

To prove Theorem
1.5
, the first difficulty one faces is the
construction of correct topology on the p-adic étale cohomology groups
with modulus on a smooth projective curve over a local field of
characteristic
p
. The technical steps involved in the definition of the
topology and proofs of some important properties span through
Sections
7
and
8
.
We prove Theorem
1.5
by combining these steps with
the Kato-Saito duality in the unramified
case, and a local duality which we deduce from Theorem
1.4
.
We finally combine our results about the
reciprocity for 2-local fields and the ramified duality theorem to
complete the proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2

in Sections
8
,
9
and
10
.
In the last section, we also prove a reciprocity theorem for the function field
of curves over a local field.

1.3.
Notations

We shall use the following notations throughout this paper.
Unless a different notation is used in a section, we shall write
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
for the
ring of integers of
K
and
{\mathfrak{m}}_{K}
for the maximal ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
if
K
is any
Henselian discrete valuation field. A local field will mean a complete discrete
valuation field with finite residue field.

For a Noetherian scheme
X
, we let
{\rm Irr}(X)
be the set of all irreducible
components of
X
. We let
X_{(q)}
denote the set of
q
-dimensional points of
X
.
For an integral scheme
X
, we let
{\rm Div}(X)
denote the group of Weil divisors on

X
.
For a scheme
X
endowed with a topology
\tau\in\{{\rm zar},{\rm nis},{\text{\'{e}t}}\}
,
a closed subset
Y\subset X
, and a

\tau
-sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
, we let
H^{*}_{\tau,Y}(X,{\mathcal{F}})
denote the
\tau
-cohomology
groups of
{\mathcal{F}}
with support in
Y
. If
\tau
is fixed in a subsection, we shall
drop it from the notations of the cohomology groups. We shall write

H^{*}_{\tau,Y}(X,{\mathcal{F}})
as
H^{*}_{\tau}(X,{\mathcal{F}})
whenever
Y=X_{\rm red}
.
If
X={\rm Spec\,}(A)
is affine, we may write
H^{*}_{\tau}(X,{\mathcal{F}})
as

H^{*}_{\tau}(A,{\mathcal{F}})
. We shall write
H^{q}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(q-1))

(resp.
H^{q}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(A,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(q-1))
) as
H^{q}(X)
(resp.
H^{q}(A)
) for
any
q\geq 0
(see
[
20
, § 3.2, Defn. 1]
).
We shall let
D_{\tau}(X)
denote the bounded derived category of sheaves on
X_{\tau}
.

If
k
is a field, we shall denote the category of
separated schemes which are of finite type over
k
by
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
.
We shall let
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
denote the category of smooth schemes over
k
.
If
{k^{\prime}}/k
is a field extension and
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
, we shall let
X_{k^{\prime}}=X\times_{{\rm Spec\,}(k)}{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})
.
For a Noetherian scheme
X
, we shall let
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(X)

denote the abelianized étale fundamental group of
X
and consider it as a
topological group with its profinite topology. We shall write
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}(F))

interchangeably as
G_{F}
, where the latter is the abelianized absolute Galois
group of
F
. We let
{\mathbf{Tab}}
denote the category of topological abelian groups
with continuous homomorphisms.

We shall let
{\mathbf{Ab}}
denote the category of abelian groups.
For
A,B\in{\mathbf{Ab}}
, the notation
A\otimes B
will indicate
tensor product of
A
and
B
over
{\mathbb{Z}}
.
For an abelian group
A
and
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we let

{}_{m}A
(resp.
A/m
) denote the kernel (resp. cokernel)
of the multiplication map
A\xrightarrow{m}A
. For a prime
p
,
we shall let
A\{p\}
(resp.
A\{p^{\prime}\}
) denote the
p
-primary (resp. prime-to-
p

primary) component of
A
.
For a set
\mathbb{M}
of prime numbers, we shall let
A_{\mathbb{M}}
denote the
inverse limit
\varprojlim_{m}A/{m}
, where the limit runs through all natural
numbers
m
whose prime divisors lie in
\mathbb{M}
. We let
A_{\rm div}={\underset{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\bigcap}}\ mA
.

2.
Reciprocity for 2-local fields I

Our goal in the next three sections is to prove Theorem
1.4
.
In this section, we shall prove some basic results concerning
relative Milnor
K
-theory,
Kato’s ramification filtration and the reciprocity homomorphism for 2-local fields.
The main technical result is Proposition
2.8
which we shall use frequently in
our proofs.

2.1.
Relative Milnor
K
-theory

For a local ring
A
, we let
A^{h}
(resp.
A^{sh}
,
\widehat{A}
) denote the
Henselization (resp. strict Henselization, completion) of
A
with respect to its
maximal ideal. If
A
is an integral domain with fraction field
K
, we let

K^{h}
(resp.
K^{sh}
,
\widehat{K}
) denote the fraction field of

A^{h}
(resp.
A^{sh}
,
\widehat{A}
).

For a local ring
A
, let
K_{n}(A)
denote the
n
-th Milnor
K
-group
of
A
as defined in
[
22
]
. For an ideal
I\subset A
,
we let
K_{n}(A,I)={\rm Ker}(K_{n}(A)\twoheadrightarrow K_{n}(A/I))
denote the relative Milnor

K
-group.
If
A
is a local integral domain with fraction field
K
and
I=(f)
is a
nonzero principal ideal, we let
A_{f}
denote the localization of
A
obtained by
inverting the powers of
f
.
We let
K_{1}(A|I)=K_{1}(A,I)
, and for
n\geq 2
, we let

K_{n}(A|I)
denote the image of the canonical map
of abelian groups

(2.1)

K_{1}(A,I)\otimes(A_{f})^{\times}\otimes\cdots\otimes(A_{f})^{\times}\to K_{n}(K),

induced by the product in Milnor
K
-theory, where the tensor product is
taken
n
times (cf.
[
39
, Definition 2.4 and Lemma 2.1]
,

[
7
, § 3]
). If
A
is a discrete valuation ring
with maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}
,
we shall write
K_{n}(A,{\mathfrak{m}}^{r})
(resp.
K_{r}(A|{\mathfrak{m}}^{r})
) as

{\rm fil}^{\prime}_{r}K_{n}(K)
(resp.
{\rm fil}_{r}K_{n}(K)
) for
n,r\geq 1
.
We shall let
{\rm fil}^{\prime}_{0}K_{n}(K)={\rm fil}_{0}K_{n}(K)=K_{n}(A)
.
One can check using
[
22
, Lem. 1]

(e.g., see
[
7
, Lem. 3.1]
and its proof) that the
following holds.

Lemma 2.1
.

For every
n,r\geq 1
, one has

{\rm fil}^{\prime}_{r}K_{n}(K)\subseteq{\rm fil}_{r}K_{n}(K)\subseteq{\rm fil}^{\prime}_{r-1}K_{n}(K)\subseteq K_{n}(A)
,
and the first inclusion is an equality when
r=1
.

Let
p>0
be a fixed prime. Let
A
be a discrete valuation ring which is an

{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-algebra and let
K
be its quotient field.
Let
m,r\geq 1
and
n\geq 0
be two integers.
We let
\kappa^{m}_{r}(A|n)=\frac{{\rm fil}_{n}K_{r}(K)}{p^{m}K_{r}(K)\cap{\rm fil}_{n}K_{r}(K)}
and define

\varpi^{m}_{r}(A|n)
so that there is an exact sequence

(2.2)

0\to\kappa^{m}_{r}(A|n)\to\kappa^{m}_{r}(A|0)\to\varpi^{m}_{r}(A|n)\to 0.

Lemma 2.2
.

One has

(1)

\kappa^{m}_{r}(A|n)=\frac{{\rm fil}_{n}K_{r}(K)}{p^{m}K_{r}(A)\cap{\rm fil}_{n}K_{r}(K)}
.

(2)

{{\rm fil}_{1}K_{r}(K)}/{p^{m}}=\kappa^{m}_{r}(A|1)=\kappa^{m}_{r}(A|0)={K_{r}(A)}/{p^{m}}
if
r\geq 2
and the residue field of
A
is a local field.

(3)

\kappa^{m}_{r}(A|n)=\frac{{\rm fil}_{n}K_{r}(K)}{p^{m}{\rm fil}_{1}K_{r}(A)\cap{\rm fil}_{n}K_{r}(K)}
under
the hypothesis of (2) if
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

To prove (1) and (2), use Lemma
2.1
,

[
19
, § 2, Lem. 1]
,
[
5
, Thm. 8.1]
and the universal
exactness of the Gersten complex for
K_{*}(A)
. The item (3) follows from
(1) and (2) because the latter implies that

p^{m}K_{r}(A)\cap{\rm fil}_{1}K_{r}(K)=p^{m}{\rm fil}_{1}K_{r}(A)
.
∎

For a closed immersion of Noetherian schemes
D\subseteq X
defined by the
sheaf of ideals
{\mathcal{I}}_{D}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
, we let
{\mathcal{K}}_{n,(X,D)}
be the sheaf
on
X_{\rm zar}
(resp.
X_{\rm nis},\ X_{\text{\'{e}t}}
) whose stalk at

x\in X
is
K_{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{X,x},{\mathcal{I}}_{D,x})
(resp.
K_{n}({\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x},{\mathcal{I}}^{h}_{D,x})
,

K_{n}({\mathcal{O}}^{sh}_{X,x},{\mathcal{I}}^{sh}_{D,x})
), where
{\mathcal{I}}_{D,x}
is the stalk of
{\mathcal{I}}_{D}
at

x
. We define the sheaves
{\mathcal{K}}_{n,X|D}
in a similar manner if
X
is integral
and
D
is locally principal.
We shall write
{\mathcal{K}}_{n,(X,D)}
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{n,X|D}
as
{\mathcal{K}}_{n,X}
when

D=\emptyset
.

For
\tau\in\{{\rm zar},{\rm nis},{\text{\'{e}t}}\}
and an
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-scheme
Y
, let

\{W_{m}\Omega^{\bullet}_{Y}\}_{m\geq 1}
be the pro-complex of the
p
-typical
de Rham-Witt sheaves on
Y_{\tau}
with the differential
d
, the Frobenius
F
,
the Verschiebung
V
, the transition map
R
and the
Teichmüller map
[-]\colon{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{Y}\to(W_{m}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y})^{\times}

(cf.
[
15
]
). Let

W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{Y,{\operatorname{log}}}={\rm Ker}(W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{Y}\xrightarrow{1-\overline{F}}{W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{Y}}/{dV^{m-1}\Omega^{r-1}_{Y}})

(cf.
[
7
, § 3.1]
). In this paper, we shall write

H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}}(j))=H^{i-j}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Y,W_{m}\Omega^{j}_{Y,{\operatorname{log}}})
and

H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n}(j))=H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Y,\mu_{n}^{\otimes j})
if
p\nmid n
.
Recall that there is a canonical map of
\tau
-sheaves

{\rm NR}_{Y}\colon{{\mathcal{K}}_{i,Y}}/n\to{\mathcal{H}}^{i}_{\tau}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n}(i))
which is the
Norm-residue homomorphism
when
p\nmid n
, and the map
{\rm dlog}\colon{{\mathcal{K}}_{i,Y}}/{p^{m}}\to W_{m}\Omega^{i}_{Y,{\operatorname{log}}}
,
\tau
-locally given by
{\rm dlog}(\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{i}\})={\rm dlog}[a_{1}]\wedge\cdots\wedge{\rm dlog}[a_{i}]
. The latter an isomorphism
when
Y
is regular (cf.
[
37
, Thm. 1.2, Cor. 4.2]
).

Given a regular
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-scheme
Y
of Krull dimension one and a divisor

D=\sum_{y}n_{y}[y]\subset Y
, we let
{\mathcal{K}}^{m}_{r,Y|D}
be the sheaf on
Y_{\tau}

whose stalks are the groups
\kappa^{m}_{r}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y_{\tau},y}|n_{y})
.
We let
W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{Y|D,{\operatorname{log}}}={\rm Image}({\rm dlog}\colon{\mathcal{K}}_{r,Y|D}\to W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{Y})
.
An easy consequence of Gersten resolutions for Milnor
K
-theory and logarithmic
de Rham-Witt sheaves is that the dlog map induces a natural isomorphism

{\rm dlog}\colon{\mathcal{K}}^{m}_{r,Y|D}\xrightarrow{\cong}W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{Y|D,{\operatorname{log}}}

in Nisnevich and étale topologies
(cf.
[
17
, Thm. 1]
,
[
28
, Thm. 2.2.2]
).
In particular, for
A
as in (
2.2
), we get

(2.3)

{\rm dlog}\colon\kappa^{m}_{r}(A|n)\xrightarrow{\cong}W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{A|n,{\operatorname{log}}}={\rm Image}({\rm dlog}\colon{\rm fil}_{n}K_{r}(K)\to W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{A}).

We shall use the following result frequently in this paper.

Lemma 2.3
.

If
A
is as in (
2.2
) and
n\geq 0,\ m,\ q\geq 1
are integers,
the following hold.

(1)

The canonical maps
{K_{2}(K)}/{p^{m}}\to{K_{2}(\widehat{K})}/{p^{m}}
and

\kappa^{m}_{2}(A|n)\to\kappa^{m}_{2}(\widehat{A}|n)
are injective.

(2)

The canonical maps

\frac{K_{2}(K)}{{\rm fil}_{n}K_{2}(K)}\to\frac{K_{2}(\widehat{K})}{{\rm fil}_{n}K_{2}(\widehat{K})}
and

\varpi^{m}_{r}(A|n)\to\varpi^{m}_{r}(\widehat{A}|n)

are bijective.

(3)

H^{n}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(R,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{q}(n-1))\to H^{n}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\widehat{R},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{q}(n-1))
is bijective if
A
is Henselian
and
R\in\{A,K\}
.

(4)

The canonical map
{K_{2}(K)}\to{K_{2}(\widehat{K})}
is injective if
A
is Henselian.

Proof.

The item (1) follows because
W_{m}\Omega^{2}_{K}\hookrightarrow W_{m}\Omega^{2}_{\widehat{K}}
.
The items (2) and the (
R=K
) case of item (3) follow from

[
21
, Lem. 1]
and
[
24
, Lem. 21]
while
(4) follows from
[
2
, Cor. 4]
.
It remains to prove (3) when
R=A
.
But this is clear if either
n\neq 2
or

p\nmid q
because of the following facts.

(1)

(A^{\times})\{p^{\prime}\}\xrightarrow{\cong}(\widehat{A}^{\times})\{p^{\prime}\}
.

(2)

{\operatorname{Br}}(A)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\operatorname{Br}}(\widehat{A})
.

(3)

\Omega^{i}_{A,{\operatorname{log}}}=\Omega^{i}_{\widehat{A},{\operatorname{log}}}=0
for
i\geq 3
.

(4)

H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(A,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})\cong H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathfrak{f}},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})
.

The (
n=2,q=p^{m}
) case follows by using the exact sequence

(2.4)

0\to H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(A,W_{m}\Omega^{2}_{A,{\operatorname{log}}})\to H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,W_{m}\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{\partial_{1}}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\mathfrak{f}},W_{m}\Omega^{1}_{{\mathfrak{f}},{\operatorname{log}}})\to 0,

which one easily deduces from
[
41
, Thms. 3.2, 4.1]
, and by
noting that
\partial_{1}
is an isomorphism by Lemma
2.3
and

[
20
, § 3, Lem. 3]

∎

2.2.
Kato topology

Since we shall use Kato’s topology extensively in this paper, we briefly
recall it here.
More details can be found in
[
19
, § 7]
(see also

[
31
, § 7.1]
).

Let
K
be a 2-local field of positive characteristic, i.e., a
complete discrete valuation field of characteristic
p>0

whose residue field
{\mathfrak{f}}
is a local field. Let
A
denote the ring of integers of

K
with maximal ideal
(\pi)
. One knows that there
is a canonical isomorphism of rings
\phi\colon{\mathfrak{f}}[[\pi]]\xrightarrow{\cong}A
. The Kato topology on
A
is the unique topology such that

\phi
is an isomorphism of topological rings when
{\mathfrak{f}}[[\pi]]
is endowed with the
product of the valuation (also called the adic) topology of
{\mathfrak{f}}
.
The Kato topology of
A^{\times}

is the subspace topology induced from that of
A
.
The Kato topology of
K^{\times}
is the unique topology which is compatible
with the group structure of
K^{\times}
and for which

A^{\times}
is open in
K^{\times}
. The Kato topology of
K_{2}(K)
is the finest
topology which is compatible with the group structures and for which
the product map
K^{\times}\times K^{\times}\to K_{2}(K)
is continuous.
This makes
K_{2}(K)
into a topological abelian group.

For any integers
m,r\geq 0
,
the Kato topology of
K_{2}(K)
induces the quotient topology on
{K_{2}(K)}/m

and the subspace topology on
{\rm fil}_{r}K_{2}(K)
. We shall use the term Kato topology
for these topologies as well.
By the Kato topologies of
\kappa^{m}_{r}(A|n)
(resp.
\varpi^{m}_{r}(A|n)
), we shall
mean the subspace topology of
\kappa^{m}_{r}(A|n)
induced from that of

{K_{2}(K)}/{p^{m}}
(resp. the
quotient topology of
\kappa^{m}_{r}(A|0)
via (
2.2
)).
Let
\partial_{K}\colon K_{2}(K)\twoheadrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}
be the Tame symbol map.
Via this surjective homomorphism, the Kato topology of
K_{2}(K)
induces
the quotient topology (called the Kato topology) on
{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}
.
On the other hand, the latter is also equipped with its valuation topology.
The following should be known to experts but we could not find a reference.

Lemma 2.4
.

The two topologies of
{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}
coincide.

Proof.

To show that the adic topology of
{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}
is finer than the Kato topology,
it suffices to show that if
U\subset{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}
is a subgroup which is
open in the Kato topology, then it contains an open subgroup in the adic topology.
To that end, we look at the composite map
T\colon K^{\times}\times K^{\times}\to K_{2}(K)\xrightarrow{\partial_{K}}{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}
. By definition,
T
is
continuous with respect to the Kato topology of
{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}
.
It follows that the map
T_{\pi}\colon K^{\times}\to{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}
,
given by
T_{\pi}(a)=\partial_{K}(\{a,\pi\})
is a continuous homomorphism.
In particular,
T_{\pi}^{-1}(U)
contains an open subgroup of
K^{\times}
.
By definition of the Kato topology of
K^{\times}
and

[
19
, § 7, Rem. 1]
,
T^{-1}_{\pi}(U)
contains a subgroup of the
form
V_{0}\times V_{1}
, where
V_{0}\subset{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}
is an open subgroup in the
adic topology and

V_{1}\subset{\underset{i\geq 1}{\prod}}{\mathfrak{f}}\pi^{i}
is an open subgroup
in the Kato topology of
\pi A\subset A
. It is clear from the
definition of
T
that
T_{\pi}(V_{0}\times V_{1})=V_{0}
which implies that

V_{0}\subset U
.

To show that the Kato topology of
{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}
is finer than the adic topology,
it is equivalent to show that
\partial_{K}
is continuous with respect to the
Kato topology on
K_{2}(K)
and adic topology on
{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}
.
Now, we look at the commutative diagram
(cf.
[
19
, § 6, Rem. 1]
)

(2.5)

\textstyle{K_{2}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{K}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{\mathfrak{f}}}
\textstyle{G_{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial^{\prime}_{K}}
\textstyle{G_{\mathfrak{f}},}

where the vertical arrows are the reciprocity homomorphisms.
It is well known and easy to show that the adic topology of
{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}

coincides with the subspace topology induced from the profinite topology of

G_{\mathfrak{f}}
via
\rho_{\mathfrak{f}}
. Since
\partial^{\prime}_{K}
is continuous and so is

\rho_{K}
(cf.
[
19
, Thm. 2, p. 304-305]
), it follows that

\partial_{K}
is also continuous.
∎

In the sequel, the default topology of
{K_{2}(K)}/n
(
n\geq 0
) and its subspaces
(e.g.,
{K_{2}(A)}/n
) will be the Kato topology, and the same for

{{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}}/n
will be the adic topology.
If
K^{\prime}
is a Henselian discrete valuation field with completion
K
, then the
Kato topology of
K^{\prime\times}
is the subspace topology induced from that
of
K^{\times}
. The Kato topology on
K_{2}(K^{\prime})
is the finest topology
such that the product map
K^{\prime\times}\times K^{\prime\times}\to K_{2}(K^{\prime})
is
continuous. It is clear that the canonical map
K_{2}(K^{\prime})\to K_{2}({K})
is
continuous.

Before we prove the next lemma, we recall some topological notions.
For
G\in{\mathbf{Ab}}
, we let
G^{\vee}={\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(G,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
.
For
G\in{\mathbf{Tab}}
, we let
G^{\star}={\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Tab}}(G,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
.
If
G
is either a profinite or a torsion locally compact Hausdorff topological
abelian group, then
G^{\star}
is a topological abelian group with its compact open
topology and satisfies the Pontryagin duality, i.e., the evaluation map

G\to(G^{*})^{*}
is an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{Tab}}
(cf.
[
38
, § 2.9]
).
For
A,B\in{\mathbf{Tab}}
, we let
{\rm Hom}_{\rm cf}(A,B)=({\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Tab}}}(A,B))_{\rm tor}
.
We shall write
{\rm Hom}_{\rm cf}(A,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
as
A^{\star}_{{\rm tor}}
.
In this paper, we shall endow
{\mathbb{Q}}
as well as the direct sums of its subgroups and
subquotients (e.g., finite abelian groups) with the discrete topology.

Lemma 2.5
.

The map
\partial_{K}\colon K_{2}(K)\twoheadrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}
induces an exact sequence

(2.6)

0\to({\mathfrak{f}}^{\times})^{\star}\to{K_{2}(K)}^{\star}\to{K_{2}(A)}^{\star}\to 0.

Proof.

Since
K_{2}(A)={\rm Ker}(\partial_{K})
, the exactness of (
2.6
)
except at
K_{2}(A)^{\star}
follows immediately from Lemma
2.4
.
To prove the exactness at
{K_{2}(A)}^{\star}
, we let

\phi_{K}\colon K_{2}(K)\to K_{2}(A)
be
given by
\phi_{K}(\{a,b\})=\{a,b\}-\{\partial_{K}(\{a,b\}),\pi\}
.
It is easily checked that
\phi_{K}
is a group homomorphism whose restriction to

K_{2}(A)
is identity. It remains to show that
\phi_{K}
is continuous with
respect to the Kato topology. Equivalently, we need to show that

\phi_{K}\colon K_{2}(K)\to K_{2}(K)
is continuous. As
\partial_{K}
is
continuous, the latter claim follows if we show that the map

\psi_{K}\colon{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}\to K_{2}(K)
is continuous, where
\psi_{K}(a)=\{a,\pi\}
. But this map is the composition of

{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}\hookrightarrow K^{\times}\to K_{2}(K)
, where the first map is the canonical
inclusion and the second map takes
a
to
\{a,\pi\}
.
The second map is clearly continuous and the first map is continuous by

[
19
, § 7, Rem. 1]
.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

The same proof as that of Lemma
2.5
shows that the sequence

(2.7)

0\to({{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}}/n)^{\star}\to({K_{2}(K)}/n)^{\star}\to({K_{2}(A)}/n)^{\star}\to 0

is exact for every
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

2.3.
The ramification filtration

We recall Kato’s ramification filtration
[
23
]
.
Let
K
be a Henselian discrete valuation field of
characteristic
p>0
with ring of integers

A
, maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}_{K}\neq 0
and residue field
{\mathfrak{f}}
.
For an integer
m\geq 1
, the Artin-Schreier-Witt sequence gives rise to an exact
sequence

(2.8)

0\to{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}}\to W_{m}(K)\xrightarrow{1-\overline{F}}W_{m}(K)\xrightarrow{\partial_{m}}H^{1}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})\to 0,

where
\overline{F}((a_{m-1},\ldots,a_{0}))=(a^{p}_{m-1},\ldots,a^{p}_{0})
.
We write
(a_{m-1},\ldots,a_{0})
as
\underline{a}
in short.
Let
v_{K}\colon K^{\times}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be the normalized valuation.
We fix integers
n\geq 0
and
m\geq 1
and let

{\rm fil}_{n}W_{m}(K)=\{\underline{a}|p^{i}v_{K}(a_{i})\geq-n\ \forall\ i\}
.
We let
{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})=\partial_{m}({\rm fil}_{n}W_{m}(K))
and

{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})={\underset{m\geq 1}{\bigcup}}\ {\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})
.
We let
{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}(K)=0
for
n<-1
and

(2.9)

{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}(K)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}H^{1}(K)\{p^{\prime}\}\bigoplus{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})&\mbox{if $n\geq 0$}\\
H^{1}(A)&\mbox{if $n=-1$}.\end{array}\right.

Let
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
(resp.
\lceil\cdot\rceil
) denote the
greatest integer (resp. smallest integer) function on
{\mathbb{N}}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

For
n\geq 0
and
\underline{a}\in W_{m}(K)
, one has
(1-\overline{F})(\underline{a})\in{\rm fil}_{n}W_{m}(K)

if and only if
\underline{a}\in{\rm fil}_{\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor}W_{m}(K)
.

Proof.

The lemma is easily deduced using induction on
m
, the exact sequence

(2.10)

0\to{\rm fil}_{n}W_{1}(K)\xrightarrow{V^{m-1}}{\rm fil}_{n}W_{m}(K)\xrightarrow{R}{\rm fil}_{\lfloor{\frac{n}{p}}\rfloor}W_{m-1}(K)\to 0,

and the fact that
(1-\overline{F})(\underline{a})\in{\rm fil}_{n}W_{m}(K)
implies

(1-\overline{F})(\underline{a^{\prime}})\in{\rm fil}_{\lfloor{\frac{n}{p}}\rfloor}W_{m-1}(K)
and

(1-\overline{F})(a_{0})\in{\rm fil}_{n}W_{1}(K)
, where
\underline{a^{\prime}}=(a_{m-1},\ldots,a_{1})
. Note that

m=1
case is trivial.
∎

A result we shall need about the ramification filtration is the
following.

Proposition 2.8
.

For every
m\geq 1
and
n\geq 0
, we have

{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})=H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})\bigcap{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})=~_{p^{m}}{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})

as subgroups of
H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})
.

Proof.

The second equality is obvious. To prove the first,
we write
{\rm fil}^{*}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})=H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})\bigcap{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})
. It is clear that
{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})\subseteq{\rm fil}^{*}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})
.
For the other inclusion, we can assume
n\geq 1
as the assertion is clear
otherwise. For
m=1
, this is already shown in the proof of

[
27
, Lem. 3.6, p. 2002]
.

We now assume
m\geq 2
, write
n^{\prime}={\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor}
and
look at the commutative diagram

(2.11)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(p^{m-1})^{*}}
\textstyle{{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R^{*}}
\textstyle{{\rm fil}_{n^{\prime}}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m-1}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm fil}^{*}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(p^{m-1})^{*}}
\textstyle{{\rm fil}^{*}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R^{*}}
\textstyle{{\rm fil}^{*}_{n^{\prime}}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m-1}})}

The arrows in the bottom row are the restrictions
of
(p^{m-1})^{*}
and
R^{*}
induced on
H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{i}})
by the
maps of sheaves
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p}\xrightarrow{p^{m-1}}{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}}\xrightarrow{R}{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m-1}}
. The exactness of the bottom row is clear and the same for
the top row is easily deduced from Lemma
2.7
and
(
2.10
).
The left and right vertical arrows are bijective by induction on
m
.
It follows that
R^{*}
on the bottom row is surjective and the middle vertical
arrow is bijective.
∎

Corollary 2.9
.

For any pair of integers
n\geq-1
and
m\geq 1
, the canonical maps

{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}(K)\to{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}(\widehat{K})
and
{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})\to{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\widehat{K},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})
are isomorphisms.

Proof.

The
n\leq 0
case of the first isomorphism is clear from
Lemma
2.3
. The general case follows
by induction using
[
23
, Cor. 3.3]
. The second isomorphism follows
by the first isomorphism, Lemma
2.3
and Proposition
2.8
.
∎

2.4.
The reciprocity homomorphism

Let
K
be a Henselian discrete valuation field of characteristic
p>0

whose residue field
{\mathfrak{f}}
is a local field.
Let
A
denote the ring of integers of
K
and
{\mathfrak{m}}=(\pi)
the maximal ideal of

A
. We let
S={\rm Spec\,}(A)
and let
x
denote the closed
point of
S
. We shall write
S_{\eta}={\rm Spec\,}(K)
and
S_{n}={\rm Spec\,}(A/{{\mathfrak{m}}^{n+1}})
for

n\geq 0
.
We let
j\colon S_{\eta}\hookrightarrow S
and
\iota\colon S_{0}\hookrightarrow S
be the inclusions.
Recall from
[
19
]
that the
reciprocity homomorphism
\rho_{K}\colon K_{2}(K)\to G_{K}
is induced by the cup
product pairing

(2.12)

K_{2}(K)\times H^{1}(K)\xrightarrow{{\rm NR}_{K}\times{\operatorname{id}}}H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(2))\times H^{1}(K)\xrightarrow{\cup}H^{3}(K)\cong{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

One knows that
\rho_{K}
is a continuous homomorphism with respect to the Kato
topology of
K_{2}(K)
and the profinite topology of
G_{K}
.
Letting
G^{(n)}_{K}=\left(\frac{H^{1}(K)}{{\rm fil}_{n-1}H^{1}(K)}\right)^{\star}

and
G^{(n)}_{K,m}=\left(\frac{H^{1}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})}{{\rm fil}_{n-1}H^{1}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{m}})}\right)^{\star}
, we have the following.

Lemma 2.10
.

For
n\geq 0
and
m\geq 1
, (
2.12
) induces continuous homomorphisms

(1)\ \rho^{n}_{K}\colon{\rm fil}_{n}K_{2}(K)\to G^{(n)}_{K};\ (2)\ \rho^{n}_{K,m}\colon\kappa^{m}_{2}(A|n)\to G^{(n)}_{K,m}.

Proof.

The existence of
\rho^{n}_{K,m}
follows from that of
\rho^{n}_{K}
using
Proposition
2.8
. For
n\geq 1
, item (1) follows from
(
2.12
) and
[
23
, Rem. 6.6]
.
The map
\rho^{0}_{K}
is the one induced between the kernels of the horizontal
arrows in (
2.5
). In particular, it is continuous.
∎

Lemma 2.11
.

For every integer
q\geq 1
, we have exact sequences

0\to{K_{2}(A)}/q\to{K_{2}(K)}/q\xrightarrow{\partial_{K}}{{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}}/q\to 0;\ \ \ 0\to{G^{(0)}_{K}}/q\to{G_{K}}/q\xrightarrow{\partial^{\prime}_{K}}{G_{\mathfrak{f}}}/q\to 0.

Proof.

The exactness of the sequence on the left is well known.
To show the exactness of the other sequence, note that

G_{\mathfrak{f}}\{p\}=G_{K}\{p\}=0
by Lemma
2.12
.
If
p\nmid q
, then one knows that
\rho_{\mathfrak{f}}\colon~_{q}{\mathfrak{f}}^{\times}\to~_{q}G_{\mathfrak{f}}
is an isomorphism. On the other
hand,
\partial_{K}
is a split surjection whose section is given by

\iota_{*}(a)=\{a,\pi\}
. It follows that
\partial_{K}
and

\partial^{\prime}_{K}
are surjective at the level of torsion subgroups. This proves our
claim.
∎

Lemma 2.12
.

If
U
is an affine
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-scheme, then
\pi^{\rm ab}_{1}(U)\{p\}=0
.

Proof.

We need to show that
H^{1}(U)
is
p
-divisible. Since

{H^{1}(U)}/p\hookrightarrow H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(U,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p})
, we are done by the Artin-Schreier exact
sequence and
[
11
, Thm. III.3.7]
.
∎

3.
A duality theorem for
\kappa^{1}_{2}(A|n)

We maintain the notations and hypotheses of §
2.4
.
In this section, we additionally assume that
A
is complete and prove a perfect
duality theorem for
\kappa^{1}_{2}(A|n)
,
a key step in the proof of Theorem
1.4
.
Since we shall only deal with
m=1
case, we shall
denote the map
\rho^{n}_{K,1}
(cf. Lemma
2.10
) by
\rho^{n}_{K}
, for brevity.

We fix some further notations.
For
Y={\rm Spec\,}(R)
and a sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}

on
Y_{\tau}
, we shall write
H^{*}_{\tau}(Y,{\mathcal{F}})
as
H^{*}_{\tau}(R,{\mathcal{F}}(Y))
.
In particular,
H^{*}_{\tau}(S,W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{S|S_{n},{\operatorname{log}}})
will be written as

H^{*}_{\tau}(A,W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{A|n+1,{\operatorname{log}}})
and so on.
We write
{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathbb{F}}_{q}((t))
and
A={\mathfrak{f}}[[\pi]]
so that

K={\mathfrak{f}}((\pi))={\mathbb{F}}_{p}((t))((\pi))
.
Thanks to the
F
-finiteness of
{\mathfrak{f}}
and
A
, one knows that

\Omega^{1}_{A}\cong Adt\bigoplus Ad\pi
and
\Omega^{1}_{A/{\mathfrak{f}}}\cong Ad\pi
.
In particular,
\Omega^{2}_{A}\cong A\omega
, where we let
\omega=dt\wedge d\pi
.
We let
\omega^{\prime}={\rm dlog}(t)\wedge{\rm dlog}(\pi)\in\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}}
.
We shall write
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(n)={\mathcal{O}}_{S}(nE)
, where
E=V((\pi))
.
For
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
M\in A
-mod, we let
A(n)=H^{0}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(S,{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(n))=\{f\in K|f=\sum_{i\geq-n}a_{i}\pi^{i}\ \mbox{with}\ a_{i}\in{\mathfrak{f}}\}
and

M(n)=M\otimes_{A}A(n)
. We let

(3.1)

\Omega^{1}_{A}({\operatorname{log}}\pi)=Adt\bigoplus A{\rm dlog}(\pi)\subset\Omega^{1}_{K},\ \Omega^{2}_{A}({\operatorname{log}}\pi)=A(dt\wedge{\rm dlog}(\pi))\subset\Omega^{2}_{K},

\Omega^{i}_{A|n}=\Omega^{i}({\operatorname{log}}\pi)(-n),\ B\Omega^{i}_{A|n}=d\Omega^{i-1}_{A|n}\ \mbox{and}\ B^{\prime}\Omega^{i}_{A|n}=\frac{\Omega^{i}_{A|n}}{d\Omega^{i-1}_{A|n}}.

We let
\psi\colon S\to S
be the absolute Frobenius. This is finite
and flat as
S
is regular.
For any
a\in K
(resp. in
{\mathfrak{f}}
), we shall denote by
\overline{a}
the residue
class of
a
in
K/A
(resp. in
{{\mathfrak{f}}}/{{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{f}}}
).
For any
A
-module
M
, we let
H^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}(M)
denote the local cohomology modules of

M
. We let
Z\Omega^{i}_{A}(n)=\Omega^{i}_{A}(n)\bigcap{\rm Ker}(d)
, where

d\colon\Omega^{i}_{K}\to\Omega^{i+1}_{K}
is the classical differential.

3.1.
Some complexes and their pairings

We now fix an integer
n\geq 0
and consider the complexes of sheaves

{\mathcal{F}}_{n}=(\psi_{*}(ZA(n))\xrightarrow{1-C}A(n));\ {\mathcal{G}}_{n}=(\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\xrightarrow{1-\overline{F}}\psi_{*}B^{\prime}\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1})
and

{\mathcal{H}}=(\Omega^{2}_{A}\xrightarrow{1-C}\Omega^{2}_{A})
in
D_{\text{\'{e}t}}(S)
.
Recall here that
C\colon H^{i}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{R})\xrightarrow{\cong}\Omega^{i}_{R}
is the Cartier operator for a regular
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-algebra
R
which
is characterized by the properties

(3.2)

C(a^{p})=a,\ C(a^{p-1}da)=da,C(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})=C(\alpha)\wedge C(\alpha^{\prime}).

It is easy to check
that the wedge product of differential forms gives rise to a pairing of complexes
(cf.
[
34
, § 1, p. 175]
,
[
17
, § 3.1]
)

(3.3)

\langle,\rangle\colon{\mathcal{F}}_{n}\times{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\to{\mathcal{H}};

\langle a,b\rangle^{0}_{0,0}=a\wedge b,\ \langle a,b\rangle^{1}_{1,0}=a\wedge b,\ \langle a,b\rangle^{1}_{0,1}=-C(a\wedge b).

On restriction to
S_{\eta}
and taking the associated
pairing of cohomology, we get a pairing

(3.4)

H^{i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)\times H^{1-i}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})\to H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{{{\rm Res}}^{\prime}_{K}}{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p,

where
{{\rm Res}}^{\prime}_{K}
is Kato’s residue (cf.
[
20
, § 3, Prop. 1]
).

Lemma 3.1
.

The pairings of finite type
A
-modules

(3.5)

A(n)\times\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\xrightarrow{\langle,\rangle^{1}_{1,0}}\Omega^{2}_{A}\cong A;\ \psi_{*}(ZA(n))\times\psi_{*}B^{\prime}\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\xrightarrow{\langle,\rangle^{1}_{0,1}}\Omega^{2}_{A}\cong A

are perfect.

Proof.

The perfectness claim for the first pairing is straightforward and the same
for the second pairing follows at once from the first
(cf.
[
34
, Lem. 1.7]
).
∎

Recall that there is a commutative diagram

(3.6)

\textstyle{H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Omega^{1}_{A/{\mathfrak{f}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{A}}
\textstyle{\frac{\Omega^{1}_{K/{\mathfrak{f}}}}{\Omega^{1}_{A/{\mathfrak{f}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{res}}^{\prime}_{K}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{f}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{\mathfrak{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{\mathfrak{f}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Omega^{2}_{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\frac{\Omega^{2}_{K}}{\Omega^{2}_{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{res}}_{K}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{f}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\frac{\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{f}}}{\Omega^{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{f}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{res}}_{\mathfrak{f}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{tr}}}

where
\gamma_{A}(\overline{a}d\pi)=\overline{a}\omega
(for
{a}\in K
) is an
isomorphism of
A
-modules while
\gamma_{\mathfrak{f}}({b})=bdt
(for
b\in{\mathfrak{f}}
) is
an isomorphism of
{\mathfrak{f}}
-modules.
The middle horizontal arrow on the top is the Poincaré residue map

\overline{a}d\pi\mapsto{\operatorname{res}}(a)
and the one below it on the bottom
is
\overline{a}\omega\mapsto{\operatorname{res}}(a)dt
, where
a\in K
.
The arrow
{\operatorname{res}}_{\mathfrak{f}}
is the residue map
\overline{b}dt\mapsto{\operatorname{res}}(b)
for
b\in{{\mathfrak{f}}}
. Here,
{\operatorname{res}}(a)
(res.
{\operatorname{res}}(b)
) denotes the classical residue of the
Laurent power series
a\in K={\mathfrak{f}}((\pi))
(resp.
b\in{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathbb{F}}_{q}((t))
).
We let
{\rm Res}_{K}\colon H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Omega^{2}_{A})\to{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p
denote the composition
of
{\operatorname{tr}}
and the bottom horizontal arrows.

We write
H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))\cong{\underset{i\geq n+1}{\bigoplus}}{\mathfrak{f}}\pi^{-i}
.
We endow each
{\mathfrak{f}}\pi^{-i}
with the adic topology of
{\mathfrak{f}}

under the canonical isomorphism
{\mathfrak{f}}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathfrak{f}}\pi^{-i}
and let

H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))
be endowed with the direct sum topology
(cf.
[
8
, § 2.6]
). This makes
H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))
into
a topological
{\mathfrak{f}}
-vector space.
We shall call this the adic topology of
H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))
.
Using the isomorphism of topological fields
{\mathfrak{f}}\xrightarrow{p}{\mathfrak{f}}^{p}

and the isomorphism
\psi_{*}(ZA(n))\cong(\psi_{*}A^{p})(\lfloor n/p\rfloor)
in
A
-mod, the above construction shows
that just like
H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))
, the
A
-module
H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\psi_{*}(ZA(n)))
is a
topological
{\mathfrak{f}}
-vector space endowed with the adic topology.

We now look at the maps

(3.7)

{\rm Hom}_{A}(M,\Omega^{2}_{A})\times H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(M)\to H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Omega^{2}_{A})\xrightarrow{{\rm Res}_{K}}{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p

for any finite type
A
-module
M
,
where the first map is induced by the composition of Ext groups of
A
-modules.
This yields the duality map
{\rm Res}^{*}_{M}\colon{\rm Hom}_{A}(M,\Omega^{2}_{A})\to{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p}}(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(M),{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)
. Letting
M\in\{A(n),\psi_{*}(ZA(n))\}

and using Lemma
3.1
, we equivalently get the maps

{\rm Res}^{*}_{n}\colon\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\to{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p}}(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n)),{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)

and
{\rm Res}^{\prime*}_{n}\colon\psi_{*}B^{\prime}\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\to{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p}}(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\psi_{*}(ZA(n))),{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)
. It is clear from
the definition of
{\rm Res}_{K}
and the adic topology of
{\mathfrak{f}}
that for every

\alpha\in\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}
, the map

{\rm Res}^{*}_{n}(\alpha)\colon{\mathfrak{f}}\pi^{-i}\to{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p

is continuous. It follows that
{\rm Res}^{*}_{n}(\alpha)\in{\rm Hom}_{\rm cont}(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n)),{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)\cong H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))^{\star}
with respect to the
adic topology of
H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))
. The same reason shows that

{\rm Res}^{\prime*}_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})\in{\rm Hom}_{\rm cont}(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\psi_{*}(ZA(n))),{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)\cong H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\psi_{*}(ZA(n)))^{\star}

for
\alpha^{\prime}\in\psi_{*}B^{\prime}\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}
.

Our first claim is the following.

Lemma 3.2
.

We have isomorphisms of
A
-modules:

{\rm Res}^{*}_{n}\colon\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\to H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))^{\star}\ \mbox{and}\ \ {\rm Res}^{\prime*}_{n}\colon\psi_{*}B^{\prime}\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\to H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\psi_{*}(ZA(n)))^{\star}.

Proof.

We only prove the first statement since the same argument applies to the second
statement too.
It is easy to verify that
{\rm Res}^{*}_{n}
is
A
-linear, where the
A
-module
structure of
H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))^{\star}
is defined by
af(x)=f(ax)
.
Now, the local Grothendieck duality for finite type
A
-modules
(cf.
[
10
, Thm. 6.3]
) and Lemma
3.1
together
imply that (
3.7
) induces an isomorphism

\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Hom}_{A}(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n)),H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Omega^{2}_{A}))
of

A
-modules. On the other hand, one easily checks using the definition of
{\operatorname{res}}_{K}

above (cf.
[
10
, Exm. 3, p. 67-68]
) that
the map
{\operatorname{res}}^{*}_{K}\colon H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Omega^{2}_{A})={\rm Hom}_{A}(A,H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Omega^{2}_{A}))\xrightarrow{\circ\ {\operatorname{res}}_{K}}{\rm Hom}_{\mathfrak{f}}(A,\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{f}})
induces an
isomorphism of
{\mathfrak{f}}
-vector spaces

{\operatorname{res}}^{*}_{K}\colon H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Omega^{2}_{A})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathfrak{f}},{\rm cont}}(A,\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{f}})
. Here,
A
is given its
{\mathfrak{m}}
-adic topology and

\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{f}}
the discrete topology.

We thus get an isomorphism

\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Hom}_{A}(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n)),{\rm Hom}_{{\mathfrak{f}},{\rm cont}}(A,\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{f}}))\cong{\rm Hom}_{A}(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n)),{\rm Hom}_{{\mathfrak{f}},{\rm cont}}(A,{\mathfrak{f}})).

Combining this with the easy-to-check isomorphisms

{\rm Hom}_{A}(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n)),{\rm Hom}_{{\mathfrak{f}},{\rm cont}}(A,{\mathfrak{f}}))={\rm Hom}_{A}(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n)),{\rm Hom}_{{\mathfrak{f}}}(A,{\mathfrak{f}}))\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Hom}_{\mathfrak{f}}(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n)),{\mathfrak{f}}),

we get that (
3.7
) with
M=A(n)
induces an isomorphism

\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Hom}_{\mathfrak{f}}(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n)),{\mathfrak{f}})
.

On the other hand, one checks using the definition of
{\operatorname{res}}_{\mathfrak{f}}
that the map

{\operatorname{res}}^{*}_{\mathfrak{f}}\colon{\rm Hom}_{\mathfrak{f}}(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n)),{\mathfrak{f}})\cong{\rm Hom}_{\mathfrak{f}}(K/{A(n)},{\mathfrak{f}})\xrightarrow{\circ\ \chi_{\mathfrak{f}}}{\rm Hom}_{\rm cont}(K/{A(n)},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)

is an isomorphism with respect to the adic topology of

{K}/{A(n)}\cong H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))
(cf.
[
44
, Chap. II, Thm. 3]
).
It follows that the map
{\rm Res}^{*}_{n}={\operatorname{res}}^{*}_{\mathfrak{f}}\circ{\operatorname{res}}^{*}_{K}\colon\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\to H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))^{*}
is an isomorphism.
∎

Our second claim is the following.

Lemma 3.3
.

We have commutative diagrams

(3.8)

\textstyle{{\Omega^{2}_{K}}/{\Omega^{2}_{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{res}}_{K}}
\textstyle{{\Omega^{2}_{K}}/{\Omega^{2}_{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{res}}_{K}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{f}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{res}}_{\mathfrak{f}}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{f}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{res}}_{\mathfrak{f}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{tr}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{tr}}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{f}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{\mathfrak{f}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p.}

Proof.

This is an elementary exercise using (
3.2
) (which implies in
particular that
C|_{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}={\operatorname{id}}
), the equalities

{\operatorname{res}}_{K}(\overline{f}\omega)={\operatorname{res}}(f)dt,\ \ {\operatorname{res}}_{\mathfrak{f}}(\overline{g}dt)={\operatorname{res}}(g)

for
f\in K
and
g\in{\mathfrak{f}}
(which implies in particular that

{\operatorname{res}}_{\mathfrak{f}}(\Omega^{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{f}}})=0
), and that the trace map in the
third diagram commutes with the Frobenius.
We leave out the details.
∎

We now consider the exact sequence of étale cohomology with support

(3.9)

H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\psi_{*}(ZA(n)))\xrightarrow{1-C}H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))\xrightarrow{\tau^{n}_{K}}{\mathbb{H}}^{2}_{x}(S,{\mathcal{F}}_{n})\to 0.

Using this exact sequence and the identities

(3.10)

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\frac{H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))}{(1-C)(H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\psi_{*}(ZA(n))))}&=&\frac{W_{1}(K)}{{\rm fil}_{n}W_{1}(K)+(1-C)(K^{p})}\\
&=&\frac{W_{1}(K)}{{\rm fil}_{n}W_{1}(K)+(1-\overline{F})(W_{1}(K))}&=&\frac{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)}{{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)},\\
\end{array}

we see that the boundary map
\partial_{1}
in (
2.8
) induces a
canonical isomorphism

(3.11)

\partial_{1}\colon{\mathbb{H}}^{2}_{x}(S,{\mathcal{F}}_{n})\xrightarrow{\cong}\frac{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)}{{\rm fil}_{n}H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)}.

As a consequence of the continuity of
C\colon H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\psi_{*}(ZA(n)))\to H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))
, the isomorphism
{\rm dlog}\colon\kappa^{1}_{2}(A|n+1)\xrightarrow{\cong}\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1,{\operatorname{log}}}
and
[
17
, Thm. 1.2.1]
, we obtain a diagram

(3.12)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\kappa^{1}_{2}(A|n+1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rho}^{n+1}_{K}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{{\rm Res}^{*}_{n+1}}

where

in (

).

Our third claim is the following.

Lemma 3.4
.

The right square in (
3.12
) is commutative.

Proof.

We let
A_{n}={K}/{A(n)}
and
B_{n}=\psi_{*}({K^{p}}/{Z(A(n))})
.
By definition, the right square in (
3.12
) is the composition
of squares

(3.13)

\textstyle{\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1-F}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta_{n}}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{A}(A_{n},{\Omega^{2}_{K}}/{\Omega^{2}_{A}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{n}}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{res}}^{*}_{K}}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{\mathfrak{f}}(A_{n},{\mathfrak{f}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p}(A_{n},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/p)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

and

(resp.

) is the duality map induced by the left

).
The commutativity of the left square in (

By the definition of

. But this the content of Lemma

.
∎

Our final claim is the following.

Lemma 3.5
.

The map
\kappa^{1}_{2}(A|n+1)\xrightarrow{\rho^{n+1}_{K}}{\mathbb{H}}^{2}_{x}(S,{\mathcal{F}}_{n})^{\vee}

factors through
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}_{x}(S,{\mathcal{F}}_{n})^{\star}
and makes the left square
in (

) commute.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.4
, it is enough to show that the left square of
(
3.12
) commutes if we replace
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}_{x}(S,{\mathcal{F}}_{n})^{\star}

(resp.
H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))^{\star}
) by
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}_{x}(S,{\mathcal{F}}_{n})^{\vee}
(resp.
H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m}}(A(n))^{\vee}
).
We now look at the diagram

(3.14)

\textstyle{\kappa^{1}_{2}(A|n+1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\rho^{n+1}_{K}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1,{\operatorname{log}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{2}_{A|n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\Omega^{2}_{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{H}}^{2}_{x}(S,{\mathcal{F}}_{n})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

in which the slanted arrows of the cube

. The map

) and

is the restriction of

It is clear that except for the back and the front,

Since the triangle on the left end commutes by the definition of

(cf. (

it suffices therefore to show that the front face of the cube commutes.

The commutativity of the front face of (

) is equivalent to that

\textstyle{H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,\Omega^{0}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})\times H^{0}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\wedge}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{F}}
\textstyle{H^{0}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(K,\Omega^{2}_{K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{C}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Res}_{K}}

where

(resp.

) is the connecting homomorphism of the cohomology sequence

(resp.

) and

)

It is standard that the diagram of wedge product pairings on the left in
